PREFACE 


The research project in connection with his D. Litt. 
dissertation on the Vedic grammatico-phonetic manuals 
(Pratisakhyas) had developed the taste in the mind of Dr. 
Siddheshwar Varma for the study of Vedic Language and 
literature in general and its linguistic aspects in particular. 
The thorough study of Vedic language and afterwards his 
inclination entirely towards linguistic brought him the 
honour of being called in the literary world “a true lover of 
knowledge, the king of Indian linguistics and the founder 
of Hindi terminology’. As one goes through the various types 
of works from the pen of Dr. Varma and comes across that 
Dr. Varma had a deep and sound knowledge of ten Indian 
languages and eleven European languages, everyone without 
any hesitation can assert that he was a linguist in real sense. 
In the field of linguistics his main endeavour was to explore 
the peculiarities of the non-literary languages and dialects. 
This was the reason that in the prime of his youth, he started 
on the most difficult and dangerous research expeditions to 
North-Western Himalayan regions at his own expenses for 
the collection of multifarious linguistic materials. In the 
series of these research expeditions Dr. Varma was enabled 
to reap the rich harvest in the form of some abstruse infor- 
mation of dialects of this area which were published from 
time to time through our Institute either in the form of 
books or research papers in Vishveshvaranand Indological 
Journal, He also discovered 27 dialects of which sixteen 
belong to Pahari group, six to Kashmiri and five to the 
Kangra valley region. He prepared the dictionaries of these 
dialects, having three parts according to the region and 
entrusted the same to Acharya Vishva Bandhu Ji, the 
Founder Director of V.V.R.L, as the Institute began to 
function as the “clearing house” for Dr. Varma voluminous 
literary writings. 


The Institute could not take up this work for publication 
for a long neriod because of its very highly technical nature 
particularly the phonetic symbols used in the work. Now, 
with the advent of modern technology in the printing, the 
Institute ventured to undertake this work for publication, 
as the present work is of great historical importence. In the 
writer’s own words “the Himalayas offer to the world not 
only a wealth of material resources but also a dialectics] 


wealth, the magnitude of which has to be evaluated by the 
coming generation (My Language hunt in_ the Himalayas, 
Transactions of the linguistic circle of Delhi, 1956 P. 6). 
Moreover, in the present repidly changing scenario, the 
preservation of non-literary language is very much essential, 
because there is imminent danger of their being lost for-ever. 
The attempt, under publication, of Dr. Varma is a land- 
mark contribution in this direction, because the book 
records linguistic materials from the very inhospitable 
terrains of Jammu and Kashmir and it is an invaluable asset 
for the linguists and historians of the world over. 


Lam very glad to present before the scholarly world, the 
publication of “Kashmiri Group” comprising six dialects 
viz Banihali (Ban.), Srinagar dia. (Sr.) Kishtawari (Kisht.), 
Balmat Koti (Balm.), Anatnaga dia. (An.), and Wampuri (W), 
compiled by Dr. Siddheshwar Varma. In the printing of 
the publication, no major change except some changes in 
phonetic symbols is made in the original press copy prepared 
under the guidance of Dr. Siddeshwar Varma. The outcome 
of this publication is a result of continuous impetus, precious 
advise from time to time and unflinching help extended by 
Dr. D. D. Sharma, a renowned linguist and a clos2 associate 
of Dr. Varma and former Professor and Head, Deptt. 
of Sanskrit, Panjab University, Chandigarh. Now it is my 
proud privilege to express my deep sense of gratitude to 
him and beseech his useful suggestions in future also to 
bring out the other unpublished works, lying with our 
Institute, of Dr. Varma fam also thankful to Sh. Jatendra 
Moudgil, Manager, Panjab University Press Chandigarh 
for his co-operation for the publication of this volume. 
I am also thankful to Shri Rakesh Kumar Sharma of 
V.V.R.L for his expert computer-composing and am also 
thankful to the Director, V.V. R.L. for his co-operation. 


T.S. BINDRA 
Professor and Chairman 


v.V.BLS & LS. Panjab University, 
Sadhu Asbram. Hoshiarpur, 
99, March, 2003. 


FORWARD 


Durign the period 1928-1942, the present writer had the 

apportunity to do some field work in a number of Kashmiri 
dialects, ranging from Kishtwar in the east to a village name 
Wampura, seven miles from Srinagar, as the sketch will indicate. 
The dialects intensively surveyed were two :- (1) Dialect of the 
Banihal Pass. The informant was Abdul Aziz, resident of the 
village Darshipora, Tehsil Ramban, District Udhampur, (2) Dialect 
of the Kashmir vally rural areas. The informant was Harijan Nath 
Kol, resident of village Wampura, seven miles from Sringagar. 
Besides the above two main dialtcts, the dialect of Srinagar was 
also roughly surveryed from time to time.The following informants 
from this city may be mentioned :-(1) Shahbana, of Barzala, 
Srinagar, (2) Brij Kishen Kaul, (3) Pt. Dina Nath Mattoo. Though 
this last gentleman elucidated to the present writer only literary 
texts, ancient and modern he had the gift of discerning the sphere 
of Kashmiri words, specifiying the sphere to which a word 
belonged, e.g. whether it was purely colloquial, or purely literary, 
or confined only to certain speech-groups, as women of certain 
religious communities. 
Besides Stingar, rapid surveys were made in some localities like 
the following (1) Balmat Kot. This village, situated in Riasii 
District, is a curious focus of two remarkable dialects, one 
pertaining to Pahari, the other to Kashmiri. These and a few more 
sub-dialects have been Tepresented in this Glossary by the 
following abbreviations:- 


Ban - Dialect of the Banihal area. 
WwW - Dialect of Wampura, near Sringar. 
Sr. - Dialect of Srinagar, including the Sringar present 


pronunciation of ancient and modern literary texts. 
Kisht - Kishtwari. 


Blam - The Kashmiri dialect of Balmat Kot. 
An - The dialect of Anantnaga.

chus means he has lit to him is, as teimischus Akh gur, he has a 
horse, lit to him is a horse, chui, thou hast, lit is to thee, as 6e chui 
Akh gur, thou hast a horse; chum. I have lit is to me as méchum 
Akhgur, I have a horse; pl. chukh, thsy have, lit to them is, as 
chukh, they have lit to them is as chukh ti'mAkh gur, they have a 
horse here the form changes with the object as W chikh, they 
have, the object being plural here as in chikh tthind z3 ‘guirj. they 
have two horses. If the object is feminine, chukh becomes chAkh 
t'mAn Akh gur, they have a more; if the owner is a single man a 
chAs, lit to him is an object f. as chAs, teimis Akh gur, he has a 
mare. Similarly chui used for the masculine object above

hast a mare. corresponding to first person as an owner viz, chum,

I no Kashmiri word begins with an audible [i]. But at the end of 


some words, a single [i] sometimes occurs, which may be taken 


KASHMIRI DIALECTS 145 


as a separate word, as in the following occurrences:- -i (Sr.), 1. 
Interj., calling someone's attention as-in Sr.. hAi, O woman ! 
look here , 2. Pronom. suffix thou. Sr ha.vAi I shall show thee, 
3. Particle, (i) only (ii) implying emphasis. Sr. bAi, I only 
(=b3+i), W sui, even he; w’'po.zui, nothing but the truth . 4. 
(colloq) (Sr.), see -e (poetieal)., But a glide [i]-j, ie an 
involunatary whishpered transitional [iJ=j, which is noticed 
neither by the speaker nor by the hearer, generally occurs with 
words beginning with some conants. In some rare cases this 
glide is not heard at all, so that a clear [I] or [i] may be heard as 
in the following occurrences and also in the words beginning with 
I (see above).
